  - [cos=0.484 src=otra-cert] A serverless forex trading application (AWS SAM) serves millions of global users 24/7. Users complain of slow login times (few minutes) and occasional HTTP 504 errors. Need to optimize performance with minimal cost . Which TWO solutions improve performance with minimal cost? A. Use Lambda@Edge to execute authentication in locations closer to users B. Deploy to multiple regions with Route 53 latency routing C. Increase CloudFront cache hit ratio with Cache-Control max-age D. Set up origin failover with origin groups E. Deploy Lambda functions in multiple regions using transit VPC ✅ A & D: Lambda@Edge + Origin failover 🎯 Lambda@Edge executes authentication closer to users globally, while origin failover prevents 504 errors when primary origin fails. ⚠️ B — Multi-region deployment has significant implementation cost ⚠️ C — Cache optimization doesn't address authentication latency ⚠️ E — Transit VPC adds complexity and cost unnecessarily 📖 Full Explanation Lambda@Edge lets you run Lambda functions to customize the content that CloudFront delivers, executing the functions in AWS locations closer to the viewer. The functions run in response to CloudFront events, without provisioning or managing servers. You can use Lambda functions to change CloudFront requests and responses at the following points: After CloudFront receives a request from a viewer (viewer request), Before CloudFront forwards the request to the origin (origin request), After CloudFront receives the response from the origin (origin response), Before CloudFront forwards the response to the viewer (viewer response). In the given scenario, you can use Lambda@Edge to allow your Lambda functions to customize the content that CloudFront delivers and to execute the authentication process in AWS locations closer to the users. In addition, you can set up an origin failover by creating an origin group with two origins with one as the primary origin and the other as the second origin which CloudFront automatically switches to when the primary origin fails. This will alleviate the occasional HTTP 504 errors that users are experiencing. The option that says: Deploy your application to multiple AWS regions to accommodate your users around the world. Set up a Route 53 record with latency routing policy to route incoming traffic to the region that provides the best latency to the user is incorrect. Although this may resolve the performance issue, this solution entails a significant implementation cost since you have to deploy your application to multiple AWS regions. Remember that the scenario asks for a solution that will improve the performance of the application with minimal cost. The option that says: Increase the cache hit ratio of your CloudFront distribution by configuring your origin to add a Cache-Control max-age directive to your objects, and specify the longest practical value for max-age is incorrect because improving the cache hit ratio for the CloudFront distribution is irrelevant in this scenario. You can improve your cache performance by increasing the proportion of your viewer requests that are served from CloudFront edge caches instead of going to your origin servers for content. However, take note that the problem in the scenario is the sluggish authentication process of your global users and not just the caching of the static objects.

  - [cos=0.473 src=otra-cert] A company runs a hybrid mobile application with a 3TB MySQL database on-premises handling write-intensive requests, connected to AWS via VPN . AWS components include Lambda functions , API Gateway , and DynamoDB . The company notices exponential Lambda costs due to 4.5-minute execution times with most time being wait time from latency to the on-premises MySQL server. User traffic is unpredictable with 20% monthly growth . Which solution should the Solutions Architect implement to reduce overall costs? A. Provision AWS Direct Connect instead of VPN to reduce network latency B. Create CloudFront distribution with API Gateway origin to cache responses C. Convert Lambda to EC2 Reserved Instances with Auto Scaling and Spot instances D. Migrate MySQL to RDS, enable API Gateway caching, optimize Lambda settings, configure DynamoDB Auto Scaling ✅ D. Migrate MySQL to RDS, enable API Gateway caching, optimize Lambda settings, configure DynamoDB Auto Scaling 🎯 Why correct: This addresses the root cause (latency) by moving the database to AWS, reduces Lambda invocations through API caching, and optimizes costs across all components. The 4.5-minute Lambda execution time is primarily wait time, so eliminating network latency between AWS Lambda and the database provides the greatest cost reduction. ⚠️ A. Direct Connect only While Direct Connect reduces latency compared to VPN, it's expensive to provision for a single application and doesn't address the fundamental issue. Keeping the database on-premises still introduces latency, and the cost of Direct Connect may exceed Lambda savings. ⚠️ B. CloudFront caching CloudFront is designed for static content caching at edge locations, not for API response caching. API Gateway has built-in caching specifically designed for API responses, making it more appropriate and cost-effective for this use case. ⚠️ C. EC2 Reserved Instances With unpredictable traffic and 20% monthly growth, Reserved Instances could be more expensive than Lambda during low-traffic periods. Lambda's pay-per-execution model is more cost-effective for variable workloads when optimized properly.
